  (1) Insufficient contact with mental health services may be a contributing factor; however, it is not known what proportion of children have sufficient contact with health professionals to allow delivery of treatment meeting minimal clinical practice guidelines, or how long children experience symptoms prior to receiving treatment.
  (2) AimsTo investigate the level of mental healthcare received by Australian children from age 4 years to 14 years.
  (3) METHOD Trajectories of mental health symptoms were mapped using the Strengths and Difficulties Questionnaire.
  (4) Health professional attendances and psychotropic medications dispensed were identified from linked national Medicare Benefits Schedule (MBS) and Pharmaceutical Benefits Scheme records.
  (5) RESULTS Four trajectories of mental health symptoms were identified (low, high-decreasing, moderate-increasing and high-increasing).
  (6) Most children with mental health symptoms had few MBS mental health attendances, and only a minority received care meeting study criteria for minimally adequate treatment.
  (7) Children in the high-increasing and moderate-increasing trajectories were more likely to access care, yet there was no evidence of improvement in symptoms.
  (8) CONCLUSIONS It is important that children and adolescents with mental health problems receive treatment that meets minimal practice guidelines.
  (9) Further research is needed to identify the quality of care currently provided to children with mental health difficulties and how clinicians can be best funded and supported to provide care meeting minimal practice guidelines.
  (10) Declaration of interestsNone.

[A5] Mental health problems of homeless children and families: longitudinal study.
  (0) OBJECTIVE To establish the mental health needs of homeless children and families before and after rehousing.
  (1) DESIGN Cross sectional, longitudinal study.
  (2) SETTING City of Birmingham.
  (3) SUBJECTS 58 rehoused families with 103 children aged 2-16 years and 21 comparison families of low socioeconomic status in stable housing, with 54 children.
  (4) MAIN OUTCOME MEASURES Children's mental health problems and level of communication; mothers' mental health problems and social support one year after rehousing.
  (5) RESULTS Mental health problems remained significantly higher in rehoused mothers and their children than in the comparison group (mothers 26% v 5%, P = 0.04; children 39% v 11%, P = 0.0003).
  (6) Homeless mothers continued to have significantly less social support at follow up.
  (7) Mothers with a history of abuse and poor social integration were more likely to have children with persistent mental health problems.
  (8) CONCLUSIONS Homeless families have a high level of complex needs that cannot be met by conventional health services and arrangements.
  (9) Local strategies for rapid rehousing into permanent accommodation, effective social support and health care for parents and children, and protection from violence and intimidation should be developed and implemented.

## Claim 693

**Claim:** Leuko-reduced blood reduces infectious complications in red blood cell transfusion.

**Obligations to label:**

- `693:o1` (core, subject): the exposure is leuko-reduced blood. Evidence must show: the study gives or measures leuko-reduced blood. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `693:o2` (core, outcome): the outcome is infectious complications. Evidence must show: the study measures infectious complications. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `693:o3` (core, relation): leuko-reduced blood reduces infectious complications. Evidence must show: infectious complications are lower with leuko-reduced blood than with non-leuko-reduced blood. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `693:o4` (qualifier, population): the population is red blood cell transfusion recipients. Evidence must show: the study includes red blood cell transfusion recipients. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5

**Abstracts:**

[A1] Clinical outcomes following institution of the Canadian universal leukoreduction program for red blood cell transfusions.
  (0) CONTEXT A number of countries have implemented a policy of universal leukoreduction of their blood supply, but the potential role of leukoreduction in decreasing postoperative mortality and infection is unclear.
  (1) OBJECTIVE To evaluate clinical outcomes following adoption of a national universal prestorage leukoreduction program for blood transfusions.
  (2) DESIGN, SETTING, AND POPULATION Retrospective before-and-after cohort study conducted from August 1998 to August 2000 in 23 academic and community hospitals throughout Canada, enrolling 14 786 patients who received red blood cell transfusions following cardiac surgery or repair of hip fracture, or who required intensive care following a surgical intervention or multiple trauma.
  (3) INTERVENTION Universal prestorage leukoreduction program introduced by 2 Canadian blood agencies.
  (4) A total of 6982 patients were enrolled during the control period and 7804 patients were enrolled following prestorage leukoreduction.
  (5) MAIN OUTCOME MEASURES All-cause in-hospital mortality and serious nosocomial infections (pneumonia, bacteremia, septic shock, all surgical site infections) occurring after first transfusion and at least 2 days after index procedure or intensive care unit admission.
  (6) Secondary outcomes included rates of posttransfusion fever and antibiotic use.
  (7) RESULTS Unadjusted in-hospital mortality rates were significantly lower following the introduction of leukoreduction compared with the control period (6.19% vs 7.03%, respectively; P =.04).
  (8) Compared with the control period, the adjusted odds of death following leukoreduction were reduced (odds ratio [OR], 0.87; 95% confidence interval [CI], 0.75-0.99), but serious nosocomial infections did not decrease (adjusted OR, 0.97; 95% CI, 0.87-1.09).
  (9) The frequency of posttransfusion fevers decreased significantly following leukoreduction (adjusted OR, 0.86; 95% CI, 0.79-0.94), as did antibiotic use (adjusted OR, 0.90; 95% CI, 0.82-0.99).
  (10) CONCLUSION A national universal leukoreduction program is potentially associated with decreased mortality as well as decreased fever episodes and antibiotic use after red blood cell transfusion in high-risk patients.

[A2] Hepatitis C virus and blood transfusion: past and present risks.
  (0) The risk of HCV transmission by blood and blood products has been greatly reduced since the early 1980's.
  (1) Selection of non-remunerated donors, donor selection to prevent HIV transmission, initial surrogate testing in some regions, and introduction of anti-HCV testing have all contributed to this.
  (2) ALT surrogate testing has become obsolete since the introduction of anti-HCV testing.
  (3) The residual risk of HCV transmission due to donations in the anti-HCV window period at present is about 1 in 100 000 transfusions of cellular products, and transmission of HCV by plasma products treated with modern inactivation methods such as solvent-detergent treatment, has not been reported.
  (4) Hemovigilance programmes, which are presently being installed, will provide more data on the safety of blood transfusion.
  (5) Introduction of HCV nucleic amplification technology (NAT) as a quality control of manufacturing pools for plasma products or as a form of blood donor screening by minipools is anticipated in many European countries for the coming year.
  (6) Given industrial developments, NAT testing of individual blood donations may become available within the next 2 years.
  (7) HCV NAT testing will further annihilate the residual risk, and the cost-effectiveness will become relatively low in comparison with other public health measures.

[A3] Margination of white blood cells in microcapillary flow.
  (0) Margination of white blood cells (WBCs) towards vessel walls is an essential precondition for their efficient adhesion to the vascular endothelium.
  (1) We perform numerical simulations with a two-dimensional blood flow model to investigate the dependence of WBC margination on hydrodynamic interactions of blood cells with the vessel walls, as well as on their collective behavior and deformability.
  (2) We find WBC margination to be optimal in intermediate ranges of red blood cell (RBC) volume fractions and flow rates, while, beyond these ranges, it is substantially attenuated.
  (3) RBC aggregation enhances WBC margination, while WBC deformability reduces it.
  (4) These results are combined in state diagrams, which identify WBC margination for a wide range of flow and cell suspension conditions.

[A4] Transmission of sporadic Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease by blood transfusion: risk factor or possible biases.
  (0) BACKGROUND The occurrence of transfusion transmissions of variant Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease (CJD) cases has reawakened attention to the possible similar risk posed by other forms of CJD.
  (1) STUDY DESIGN AND METHODS CJD with a definite or probable diagnosis (sporadic CJD, n = 741; genetic CJD, n = 175) and no-CJD patients with definite alternative diagnosis (n = 482) with available blood transfusion history were included in the study.
  (2) The risk of exposure to blood transfusion occurring more than 10 years before disease onset and for some possible confounding factors was evaluated by calculating crude odds ratios (ORs).
  (3) Variables with significant ORs in univariate analyses were included in multivariate logistic regression analyses.
  (4) RESULTS In the univariate model, blood transfusion occurring more than 10 years before clinical onset is 4.1-fold more frequent in sporadic CJD than in other neurologic disorders.
  (5) This significance is lost when the 10-year lag time was not considered.
  (6) Multivariate analyses show that the risk of developing sporadic CJD after transfusion increases (OR, 5.05) after adjusting for possible confounding factors.
  (7) Analysis conducted on patients with genetic CJD did not reveal any significant risk factor associated with transfusion.
  (8) CONCLUSION This is the first case-control study showing a significant risk of transfusion occurring more than 10 years before clinical onset in sporadic CJD patients.
  (9) It remains questionable whether the significance of these data is biologically plausible or the consequence of biases in the design of the study, but they counterbalance previous epidemiologic negative reports that might have overestimated the assessment of blood safety in sporadic CJD.

[A5] Centrifugal elutriation as a method for isolation of large numbers of functionally intact human peripheral blood monocytes.
  (0) Centrifugal elutriation was used further to isolate human peripheral blood monocytes (HPBM) from mononuclear-enriched cells harvested as a secondary component following platelet concentration collection samples.
  (1) HPBM were recovered in either one or two populations consisting of either total HPBM or small (SM) and large monocytes (LM).
  (2) The elutriation was carried out at 3,500 +/-
  (3) 5 rpm for the separation of lymphocytes and HPBM in Ca++- and Mg++-free PBS without EDTA.
  (4) An average of 5.05 +/-
  (5) 1.50 X 10(8) HPBM were recovered in the total HPBM with a purity of 95% +/-
  (6) 3%.
  (7) The SM and LM were obtained by splitting the total HPBM into two equal populations with an HPBM purity of 92% +/-
  (8) 3% and 93% +/-
  (9) 3, respectively, by nonspecific esterase staining.
  (10) The elutriation media were shown to have no effect on viability by trypan blue exclusion.
  (11) All three HPBM populations were shown to be histochemically (lack of reactivity to leu-1 and leu-7) and functionally (depletion of NK cell activity) purified from the lymphocyte population.
  (12) The HPBM populations were enriched in HLA-Dr, OKM-1, OKM-5, MY-8, and leu M-3 monoclonal antibody marker staining.
  (13) There were no differences in percent positive cells between SM and LM populations for any of the monocyte-specific monoclonal antibodies.
  (14) All three monocyte populations mediated antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity to human red blood cells, with LM mediating more lysis (27.0% +/-
  (15) 5%) than SM (7% +/-
  (16) 3%).(ABSTRACT TRUNCATED AT 250 WORDS)

## Claim 700

**Claim:** Localization of PIN1 in the Arabidopsis embryo does not require VPS9a

**Obligations to label:**

- `700:o1` (core, subject): the subject is PIN1 localization in the Arabidopsis embryo. Evidence must show: the study identifies where PIN1 is located in the Arabidopsis embryo. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `700:o2` (core, relation): PIN1 localization does not require VPS9a. Evidence must show: the study shows PIN1 localizes without VPS9a. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `700:o3` (core, outcome): the outcome is PIN1 localization. Evidence must show: the study measures PIN1 localization in the embryo. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5

**Abstracts:**

[A1] Local, Efflux-Dependent Auxin Gradients as a Common Module for Plant Organ Formation
  (0) Plants, compared to animals, exhibit an amazing adaptability and plasticity in their development.
  (1) This is largely dependent on the ability of plants to form new organs, such as lateral roots, leaves, and flowers during postembryonic development.
  (2) Organ primordia develop from founder cell populations into organs by coordinated cell division and differentiation.
  (3) Here, we show that organ formation in Arabidopsis involves dynamic gradients of the signaling molecule auxin with maxima at the primordia tips.
  (4) These gradients are mediated by cellular efflux requiring asymmetrically localized PIN proteins, which represent a functionally redundant network for auxin distribution in both aerial and underground organs.
  (5) PIN1 polar localization undergoes a dynamic rearrangement, which correlates with establishment of auxin gradients and primordium development.
  (6) Our results suggest that PIN-dependent, local auxin gradients represent a common module for formation of all plant organs, regardless of their mature morphology or developmental origin.

[A2] Generation of cell polarity in plants links endocytosis, auxin distribution and cell fate decisions
  (0) Dynamically polarized membrane proteins define different cell boundaries and have an important role in intercellular communication—a vital feature of multicellular development.
  (1) Efflux carriers for the signalling molecule auxin from the PIN family are landmarks of cell polarity in plants and have a crucial involvement in auxin distribution-dependent development including embryo patterning, organogenesis and tropisms.
  (2) Polar PIN localization determines the direction of intercellular auxin flow, yet the mechanisms generating PIN polarity remain unclear.
  (3) Here we identify an endocytosis-dependent mechanism of PIN polarity generation and analyse its developmental implications.
  (4) Real-time PIN tracking showed that after synthesis, PINs are initially delivered to the plasma membrane in a non-polar manner and their polarity is established by subsequent endocytic recycling.
  (5) Interference with PIN endocytosis either by auxin or by manipulation of the Arabidopsis Rab5 GTPase pathway prevents PIN polarization.
  (6) Failure of PIN polarization transiently alters asymmetric auxin distribution during embryogenesis and increases the local auxin response in apical embryo regions.
  (7) This results in ectopic expression of auxin pathway-associated root-forming master regulators in embryonic leaves and promotes homeotic transformation of leaves to roots.
  (8) Our results indicate a two-step mechanism for the generation of PIN polar localization and the essential role of endocytosis in this process.
  (9) It also highlights the link between endocytosis-dependent polarity of individual cells and auxin distribution-dependent cell fate establishment for multicellular patterning.

[A3] The VTI family of SNARE proteins is necessary for plant viability and mediates different protein transport pathways.
  (0) The Arabidopsis genome contains a family of v-SNAREs: VTI11, VTI12, and VTI13.
  (1) Only VTI11 and VTI12 are expressed at appreciable levels.
  (2) Although these two proteins are 60% identical, they complement different transport pathways when expressed in the yeast vti1 mutant.
  (3) VTI11 was identified recently as the mutated gene in the shoot gravitropic mutant zig.
  (4) Here, we show that the vti11 zig mutant has defects in vascular patterning and auxin transport.
  (5) An Arabidopsis T-DNA insertion mutant, vti12, had a normal phenotype under nutrient-rich growth conditions.
  (6) However, under nutrient-poor conditions, vti12 showed an accelerated senescence phenotype, suggesting that VTI12 may play a role in the plant autophagy pathway.
  (7) VTI11 and VTI12 also were able to substitute for each other in their respective SNARE complexes, and a double-mutant cross between zig and vti12 was embryo lethal.
  (8) These results suggest that some VTI1 protein was necessary for plant viability and that the two proteins were partially functionally redundant.

[A4] A molecular framework for plant regeneration.
  (0) Plants and some animals have a profound capacity to regenerate organs from adult tissues.
  (1) Molecular mechanisms for regeneration have, however, been largely unexplored.
  (2) Here we investigate a local regeneration response in Arabidopsis roots.
  (3) Laser-induced wounding disrupts the flow of auxin-a cell-fate-instructive plant hormone-in root tips, and we demonstrate that resulting cell-fate changes require the PLETHORA, SHORTROOT, and SCARECROW transcription factors.
  (4) These transcription factors regulate the expression and polar position of PIN auxin efflux-facilitating membrane proteins to reconstitute auxin transport in renewed root tips.
  (5) Thus, a regeneration mechanism using embryonic root stem-cell patterning factors first responds to and subsequently stabilizes a new hormone distribution.

[A5] AIR-2: An Aurora/Ipl1-related Protein Kinase Associated with Chromosomes and Midbody Microtubules Is  Required for Polar Body Extrusion and Cytokinesis  in Caenorhabditis elegans Embryos
  (0) An emerging family of kinases related to the Drosophila Aurora and budding yeast Ipl1 proteins has been implicated in chromosome segregation and mitotic spindle formation in a number of organisms.
  (1) Unlike other Aurora/Ipl1-related kinases, the Caenorhabditis elegans orthologue, AIR-2, is associated with meiotic and mitotic chromosomes.
  (2) AIR-2 is initially localized to the chromosomes of the most mature prophase I–arrested oocyte residing next to the spermatheca.
  (3) This localization is dependent on the presence of sperm in the spermatheca.
  (4) After fertilization, AIR-2 remains associated with chromosomes during each meiotic division.
  (5) However, during both meiotic anaphases, AIR-2 is present between the separating chromosomes.
  (6) AIR-2 also remains associated with both extruded polar bodies.
  (7) In the embryo, AIR-2 is found on metaphase chromosomes, moves to midbody microtubules at anaphase, and then persists at the cytokinesis remnant.
  (8) Disruption of AIR-2 expression by RNA- mediated interference produces entire broods of one-cell embryos that have executed multiple cell cycles in the complete absence of cytokinesis.
  (9) The embryos accumulate large amounts of DNA and microtubule asters.
  (10) Polar bodies are not extruded, but remain in the embryo where they continue to replicate.
  (11) The cytokinesis defect appears to be late in the cell cycle because transient cleavage furrows initiate at the proper location, but regress before the division is complete.
  (12) Additionally, staining with a marker of midbody microtubules revealed that at least some of the components of the midbody are not well localized in the absence of AIR-2 activity.
  (13) Our results suggest that during each meiotic and mitotic division, AIR-2 may coordinate the congression of metaphase chromosomes with the subsequent events of polar body extrusion and cytokinesis.

## Claim 729

**Claim:** Lymphadenopathy is observed in knockin mouse lacking the SHP-2 MAPK pathway.

**Obligations to label:**

- `729:o1` (core, subject): the subject is a knockin mouse lacking the SHP-2 MAPK pathway. Evidence must show: the study creates or identifies a knockin mouse lacking the SHP-2 MAPK pathway. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `729:o2` (core, outcome): the outcome is lymphadenopathy. Evidence must show: the study observes lymphadenopathy in the mouse model. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `729:o3` (core, relation): lymphadenopathy is observed in the mouse model. Evidence must show: the study confirms the presence of lymphadenopathy in the knockin mouse. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5

**Abstracts:**

[A1] Control of CNS Cell-Fate Decisions by SHP-2 and Its Dysregulation in Noonan Syndrome
  (0) Within the developing mammalian CNS, growth factors direct multipotent precursors to generate neurons versus glia, a process that if perturbed might lead to neural dysfunction.
  (1) In this regard, genetic mutations resulting in constitutive activation of the protein tyrosine phosphatase SHP-2 cause Noonan Syndrome (NS), which is associated with learning disabilities and mental retardation.
  (2) Here, we demonstrate that genetic knockdown of SHP-2 in cultured cortical precursors or in the embryonic cortex inhibited basal neurogenesis and caused enhanced and precocious astrocyte formation.
  (3) Conversely, expression of an NS SHP-2 mutant promoted neurogenesis and inhibited astrogenesis.
  (4) Neural cell-fate decisions were similarly perturbed in a mouse knockin model that phenocopies human NS.
  (5) Thus, SHP-2 instructs precursors to make neurons and not astrocytes during the neurogenic period, and perturbations in the relative ratios of these two cell types upon constitutive SHP-2 activation may contribute to the cognitive impairments in NS patients.

[A2] Dissection of signaling cascades through gp130 in vivo: reciprocal roles for STAT3- and SHP2-mediated signals in immune responses.
  (0) We generated a series of knockin mouse lines, in which the cytokine receptor gp130-dependent STAT3 and/or SHP2 signals were disrupted, by replacing the mouse gp130 gene with human gp130 mutant cDNAs.
  (1) The SHP2 signal-deficient mice (gp130F759/F759 were born normal but displayed splenomegaly and lymphadenopathy and an enhanced acute phase reaction.
  (2) In contrast, the STAT3 signal-deficient mice (gp130FXQ/FXXQ) died perinatally, like the gp130-deficient mice (gp130D/D).
  (3) The gp130F759/F759 mice showed prolonged gp130-induced STAT3 activation, indicating a negative regulatory role for SHP2.
  (4) Th1-type cytokine production and IgG2a and IgG2b production were increased in the gp130F759/F759 mice, while they were decreased in the gp130FXXQ/FXXQ immune system.
  (5) These results indicate that the balance of positive and negative signals generated through gp130 regulates the immune responses.

[A3] Roles of induced expression of MAPK phosphatase-2 in tumor development in RET-MEN2A transgenic mice
  (0) Germline mutations in the RET tyrosine kinase gene are responsible for the development of multiple endocrine neoplasia 2A and 2B (MEN2A and MEN2B).
  (1) However, knowledge of the fundamental principles that determine the mutant RET-mediated signaling remains elusive.
  (2) Here, we report increased expression of mitogen-activated protein kinase phosphatase-2 (MKP-2) in carcinomas developed in transgenic mice carrying RET with the MEN2A mutation (RET-MEN2A).
  (3) The expression of MKP-2 was not only induced by RET-MEN2A or RET-MEN2B mutant proteins but also by the activation of endogenous RET by its ligand, glial cell line-derived neurotrophic factor (GDNF).
  (4) MKP-2 expression was also evident in the MKK-f cell line, which was established from a mammary tumor developed in a RET-MEN2A transgenic mouse.
  (5) Inhibition of MKP-2 attenuated the in vitro and in vivo proliferation of MKK-f cells, which was mediated by the suppression of cyclin B1 expression.
  (6) Furthermore, we found that MKP-2 is highly expressed in medullary thyroid carcinomas derived from MEN2A patients.
  (7) These findings suggest that the increased expression of MKP-2 may play a crucial role in oncogenic signaling downstream of mutant RET, leading to deregulation of cell cycle.

[A4] Integration of Smad and MAPK pathways: a link and a linker revisited.
  (0) Cells develop by reading mixed signals.
  (1) Nowhere is this clearer than in the highly dynamic processes that propel embryogenesis, when critical cell-fate decisions are made swiftly in response to well-orchestrated growthfactor combinations.
  (2) Learning how diverse signaling pathways are integrated is therefore essential for understanding physiology.
  (3) This requires the identification, in tangible molecular terms, of key nodes for pathway integration that operate in vivo.
  (4) A report in this issue, on the integration of Smad and Ras/MAPK pathways during neural induction (Pera et al. 2003), provides timely insights into the relevance of one such node.
  (5) Pera et al. (2003) report that FGF8 and IGF2—two growth factors that activate the Ras/MAPK pathway— favor neural differentiation and mesoderm dorsalization in Xenopus by inhibiting BMP (Bone Morphogenetic Protein) signaling.
  (6) Mesoderm is formed from ectoderm in response to Nodal-related signals from the endoderm at the blastula stage and beyond (Fig. 1; for review, see De Robertis et al. 2000).
  (7) BMP induces differentiation of ectoderm into epidermal cell fates at the expense of neural fates, and it ventralizes the mesoderm at the expense of dorsal fates (for review, see Weinstein and HemmatiBrivanlou 1999; De Robertis et al. 2000).
  (8) Accordingly, neural differentiation and dorsal mesoderm formation are favored when BMP signaling is attenuated.
  (9) Noggin, Chordin, Cerberus, and Follistatin, secreted by the Spemann organizer on the dorsal side at the gastrula stage, facilitate the formation of neural tissue by sequestering BMP (Weinstein and Hemmati-Brivanlou 1999; De Robertis et al. 2000).
  (10) Experimentally blocking BMP signaling with a dominant-negative BMP receptor has a similar effect of promoting ectoderm neuralization (Weinstein and Hemmati-Brivanlou 1999).
  (11) As it turns out, neural induction can also be achieved with FGF (fibroblast growth factor; Kengaku and Okamoto 1993; Lamb and Harland 1995; Hongo et al. 1999; Hardcastle et al. 2000; Streit et al. 2000; Wilson et al. 2000) and IGF (insulin-like growth factor; Pera et al. 2001; Richard-Parpaillon et al. 2002).
  (12) Injection of transcripts encoding FGF8 or IFG2 into one animal-pole blastomere of a fourto eight-cell embryo results in an expanded neural plate at the injected side (Pera et al. 2003).
  (13) Surprisingly, expression of a dominant-negative FGF receptor prevents neuralization of ectoderm explants by the BMP blocker Noggin (Launay et al. 1996).
  (14) Likewise, the potent neuralizing effect of Chordin can be blocked by a dominant-negative FGF receptor or a morpholino oligonucleotide targeting the IGF receptor (Pera et al. 2003).
  (15) Thus, the neuralizing effect of BMP inhibitors is somehow tied to FGF and IFG signaling.
  (16) The question is, how?
  (17) Because FGF8 and IFG2 activate MAPK, Pera et al. (2003) took heed from previous work showing that MAPK inhibits the BMP signal-transduction factor Smad1 (Kretzschmar et al. 1997a).
  (18) Smad1 is directly phosphorylated by the BMP receptor, resulting in Smad1 activation (Kretzschmar et al. 1997b), and by MAPK in response to EGF, resulting in Smad1 inhibition (Kretzschmar et al. 1997a; Fig. 2).
  (19) Smad transcription factors mediate gene responses to the entire TGF (Transforming Growth Factor) family, to which the BMPs belong (for review, see Massague 2000; Derynck and Zhang 2003).
  (20) Smads 1, 5, and 8 act primarily downstream of BMP receptors and Smads 2 and 3 downstream of TGF , Activin and Nodal receptors.
  (21) Smad proteins have two conserved globular domains—the MH1 and MH2 domains (Fig. 2).
  (22) The MH1 domain is involved in DNA binding and the MH2 domain in binding to cytoplasmic retention factors, activated receptors, nucleoporins in the nuclear pore, and DNA-binding cofactors, coactivators, and corepressors in the nucleus (for review, see Shi and Massague 2003).
  (23) Receptor-mediated phosphorylation occurs at the carboxy-terminal sequence SXS.
  (24) This enables the nuclear accumulation of Smads and their association with the shared partner Smad4 to form transcriptional complexes that are interpreted by the cell as a function of the context (Massague 2000).
  (25) Between the MH1 and MH2 domains lies a linker region of variable sequence and length.
  (26) Attention was drawn to this region when it was found that EGF (epidermal growth factor), a classical activator of the Ras/ MAPK pathway, causes phosphorylation of the Smad1 linker at four MAPK sites (PXSP sequences; Kretzschmar et al. 1997a).
  (27) This prevents the nuclear localization of Smad1 and inhibits BMP signaling.
  (28) Mutation of these E-MAIL j-massague@ski.mskcc.org; FAX (212) 717-3298.
  (29) Article and publication are at http://www.genesdev.org/cgi/doi/10.1101/ gad.1167003.

[A5] Defective replication stress response inhibits lymphomagenesis and impairs lymphocyte reconstitution
  (0) DNA replication stress promotes genome instability in cancer.
  (1) However, the contribution of the replication stress response to the development of malignancies remains unresolved.
  (2) The DNA replication stress response protein SMARCAL1 stabilizes DNA replication forks and prevents replication fork collapse, a cause of DNA breaks and apoptosis.
  (3) While the fork regression/remodeling functions of SMARCAL1 have been investigated, its in vivo functions in replication stress and cancer are unclear.
  (4) Using a gamma radiation (IR)-induced replication stress T-cell lymphoma mouse model, we observed a significant inhibition of lymphomagenesis in mice lacking one or both alleles of Smarcal1.
  (5) Notably, a quarter of the Smarcal1-deficient mice did not develop tumors.
  (6) Moreover, hematopoietic stem/progenitor cells (HSPCs) and developing thymocytes in Smarcal1-deficient mice showed increased DNA damage and apoptosis during the proliferation burst following IR and an impaired ability to repopulate the thymus after IR.
  (7) Additionally, mice lacking Smarcal1 showed significant HSPC defects when challenged to respond to other replication stress stimuli.
  (8) Thus, our data reveal the critical function of the DNA replication stress response and, specifically, Smarcal1 in hematopoietic cell survival and tumor development.
  (9) Our results also provide important insight into the immunodeficiency observed in individuals with mutations in SMARCAL1 by suggesting that it is an HSPC defect.

## Claim 756

**Claim:** Many proteins in human cells can be post-translationally modified at lysine residues via acetylation.

**Obligations to label:**

- `756:o1` (core, subject): the subject is proteins in human cells. Evidence must show: the study identifies proteins in human cells. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `756:o2` (core, outcome): the outcome is post-translational modification at lysine residues. Evidence must show: the study measures post-translational modification at lysine residues. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `756:o3` (core, relation): the modification is via acetylation. Evidence must show: the study shows acetylation as the modification method. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5

**Abstracts:**

[A1] The growing landscape of lysine acetylation links metabolism and cell signalling
  (0) Lysine acetylation is a conserved protein post-translational modification that links acetyl-coenzyme A metabolism and cellular signalling.
  (1) Recent advances in the identification and quantification of lysine acetylation by mass spectrometry have increased our understanding of lysine acetylation, implicating it in many biological processes through the regulation of protein interactions, activity and localization.
  (2) In addition, proteins are frequently modified by other types of acylations, such as formylation, butyrylation, propionylation, succinylation, malonylation, myristoylation, glutarylation and crotonylation.
  (3) The intricate link between lysine acylation and cellular metabolism has been clarified by the occurrence of several such metabolite-sensitive acylations and their selective removal by sirtuin deacylases.
  (4) These emerging findings point to new functions for different lysine acylations and deacylating enzymes and also highlight the mechanisms by which acetylation regulates various cellular processes.

[A2] BRIEFINGS IN FUNCTIONAL GENOMICS AND PROTEOMICS. VOL 5. NO 3. 209^221 doi:10.1093/bfgp/ell028 Histone acetylation in gene regulation
  (0) Genetic information is packaged in the highly dynamic nucleoprotein structure called chromatin.
  (1) Many biological processes are regulated via post-translational modifications of key proteins.
  (2) Acetylation of lysine residues at the N-terminal histone tails is one of the most studied covalent modifications influencing gene regulation in eukaryotic cells.
  (3) This review focuses on the role of enzymes involved in controlling both histone and non-histone proteins acetylation levels in the cell, with particular emphasis on their effects on cancer.

[A3] Identification and Functional Characterization of N-Terminally Acetylated Proteins in Drosophila melanogaster
  (0) Protein modifications play a major role for most biological processes in living organisms.
  (1) Amino-terminal acetylation of proteins is a common modification found throughout the tree of life: the N-terminus of a nascent polypeptide chain becomes co-translationally acetylated, often after the removal of the initiating methionine residue.
  (2) While the enzymes and protein complexes involved in these processes have been extensively studied, only little is known about the biological function of such N-terminal modification events.
  (3) To identify common principles of N-terminal acetylation, we analyzed the amino-terminal peptides from proteins extracted from Drosophila Kc167 cells.
  (4) We detected more than 1,200 mature protein N-termini and could show that N-terminal acetylation occurs in insects with a similar frequency as in humans.
  (5) As the sole true determinant for N-terminal acetylation we could extract the (X)PX rule that indicates the prevention of acetylation under all circumstances.
  (6) We could show that this rule can be used to genetically engineer a protein to study the biological relevance of the presence or absence of an acetyl group, thereby generating a generic assay to probe the functional importance of N-terminal acetylation.
  (7) We applied the assay by expressing mutated proteins as transgenes in cell lines and in flies.
  (8) Here, we present a straightforward strategy to systematically study the functional relevance of N-terminal acetylations in cells and whole organisms.
  (9) Since the (X)PX rule seems to be of general validity in lower as well as higher eukaryotes, we propose that it can be used to study the function of N-terminal acetylation in all species.

[A4] Generation and characterization of methyl-lysine histone antibodies.
  (0) Publisher Summary This chapter summarizes the development and characterization of rabbit polyclonal antibodies named histone that are directed against the methylated H3-K9 position.
  (1) It provides protocols for peptide design, rabbit immunizations, and quality controls of methyl-lysine histone antibodies, followed by their in vivo characterization using indirect IF of inter-and metaphase chromatin in wild-type (wt) and mutant mouse cells that are deficient for the Suv39h histone methyltransferases (HMTases).
  (2) Histone amino-termini (tails) protrude from the nucleosome core and are subject to a variety of post-translational modifications, including acetylation (on lysine residues), phosphorylation (on serine and threonine residues), methylation (on lysine and arginine residues), ubiquitination (on lysine residues), and ADP-ribosylation (on glutamic acid residues).
  (3) In addition to their structural roles, histones play important functions in the control of gene expression by regulating access to the underlying nucleosomal template.
  (4) It is without doubt that the development of high-quality, position-specific methyl-lysine histone antibodies can provide important tools for the further decoding of the epigenetic information, which is in part, indexed by distinct methylation states of selective lysine residues in the histone amino-termini.
  (5) A comparative analysis indicates significant discrepancies in the specificity and avidity of the available methyl-lysine histone antibodies and highlights the need for extensive quality controls, such that experimental data can be correctly interpreted despite the exquisite complexity of histone lysine methylation.

[A5] Post-translational modifications of the beta-1 subunit of AMP-activated protein kinase affect enzyme activity and cellular localization.
  (0) The AMP-activated protein kinase (AMPK) is a ubiquitous mammalian protein kinase important in the adaptation of cells to metabolic stress.
  (1) The enzyme is a heterotrimer, consisting of a catalytic alpha subunit and regulatory beta and gamma subunits, each of which is a member of a larger isoform family.
  (2) The enzyme is allosterically regulated by AMP and by phosphorylation of the alpha subunit.
  (3) The beta subunit is post-translationally modified by myristoylation and multi-site phosphorylation.
  (4) In the present study, we have examined the impact of post-translational modification of the beta-1 subunit on enzyme activity, heterotrimer assembly and subcellular localization, using site-directed mutagenesis and expression of subunits in mammalian cells.
  (5) Removal of the myristoylation site (G2A mutant) results in a 4-fold activation of the enzyme and relocalization of the beta subunit from a particulate extranuclear distribution to a more homogenous cell distribution.
  (6) Mutation of the serine-108 phosphorylation site to alanine is associated with enzyme inhibition, but no change in cell localization.
  (7) In contrast, the phosphorylation site mutations, SS24, 25AA and S182A, while having no effects on enzyme activity, are associated with nuclear redistribution of the subunit.
  (8) Taken together, these results indicate that both myristoylation and phosphorylation of the beta subunit of AMPK modulate enzyme activity and subunit cellular localization, increasing the complexity of AMPK regulation.

## Claim 775

**Claim:** Mice defective for deoxyribonucleic acid (DNA) polymerase I (polI) reveal increased sensitivity to ionizing radiation (IR).

**Obligations to label:**

- `775:o1` (core, subject): the subject is mice defective for DNA polymerase I (polI). Evidence must show: the study examines mice with DNA polymerase I deficiency. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `775:o2` (core, outcome): the outcome is increased sensitivity to ionizing radiation. Evidence must show: the study measures increased sensitivity to ionizing radiation. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `775:o3` (core, relation): deficiency in DNA polymerase I leads to increased sensitivity to ionizing radiation. Evidence must show: sensitivity to ionizing radiation is higher in DNA polymerase I deficient mice. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5

**Abstracts:**

[A1] Deregulated DNA polymerase β strengthens ionizing radiation-induced nucleotidic and chromosomal instabilities
  (0) DNA polymerase β (Pol β) is an error-prone enzyme which has been found to be overexpressed in several human tumors.
  (1) By using a couple of recombinant CHO cells differing only from the exogenous expression of Pol β, we showed here that cells overexpressing Pol β are much more sensitive to IR treatments by increasing apoptosis.
  (2) We also found that the surviving cells displayed an hypermutator phenotype which could be explained by different pathways involving Pol β, such as (i) an increased capacity to incorporate into DNA the mutagenic dGTP analog, 8-oxo-dGTP, one of the most abundant purine-derived nucleotides exposed to γ-irradiation, (ii) the induction of IR-induced DNA breaks and (iii) accumulation of chromosome aberrations induced by radiation.
  (3) Alteration of Pol β expression in irradiated cells thus appears to strengthen both cell death and genetic changes associated with a malignant phenotype.
  (4) These data provide new insights into the cellular response to radiations and the associated carcinogenic consequences.

[A2] Association of DNA polymerase mu (pol mu) with Ku and ligase IV: role for pol mu in end-joining double-strand break repair.
  (0) Mammalian DNA polymerase mu (pol mu) is related to terminal deoxynucleotidyl transferase, but its biological role is not yet clear.
  (1) We show here that after exposure of cells to ionizing radiation (IR), levels of pol mu protein increase.
  (2) pol mu also forms discrete nuclear foci after IR, and these foci are largely coincident with IR-induced foci of gammaH2AX, a previously characterized marker of sites of DNA double-strand breaks.
  (3) pol mu is thus part of the cellular response to DNA double-strand breaks.
  (4) pol mu also associates in cell extracts with the nonhomologous end-joining repair factor Ku and requires both Ku and another end-joining factor, XRCC4-ligase IV, to form a stable complex on DNA in vitro.
  (5) pol mu in turn facilitates both stable recruitment of XRCC4-ligase IV to Ku-bound DNA and ligase IV-dependent end joining.
  (6) In contrast, the related mammalian DNA polymerase beta does not form a complex with Ku and XRCC4-ligase IV and is less effective than pol mu in facilitating joining mediated by these factors.
  (7) Our data thus support an important role for pol mu in the end-joining pathway for repair of double-strand breaks.

[A3] The DNA polymerase λ is required for the repair of non-compatible DNA double strand breaks by NHEJ in mammalian cells
  (0) DNA polymerase lambda (polλ) is a recently identified DNA polymerase whose cellular function remains elusive.
  (1) Here we show, that polλ participates at the molecular level in a chromosomal context, in the repair of DNA double strand breaks (DSB) via non-homologous end joining (NHEJ) in mammalian cells.
  (2) The expression of a catalytically inactive form of polλ (polλDN) decreases the frequency of NHEJ events in response to I-Sce-I-induced DSB whereas inactivated forms of its homologues polβ and polμ do not.
  (3) Only events requiring DNA end processing before ligation are affected; this defect is associated with large deletions arising in the vicinity of the induced DSB.
  (4) Furthermore, polλDN-expressing cells exhibit increased sensitization and genomic instability in response to ionizing radiation similar to that of NHEJ-defective cells.
  (5) Our data support a requirement for polλ in repairing a subset of DSB in genomic DNA, thereby contributing to the maintenance of genetic stability mediated by the NHEJ pathway.

[A4] Defective replication stress response inhibits lymphomagenesis and impairs lymphocyte reconstitution
  (0) DNA replication stress promotes genome instability in cancer.
  (1) However, the contribution of the replication stress response to the development of malignancies remains unresolved.
  (2) The DNA replication stress response protein SMARCAL1 stabilizes DNA replication forks and prevents replication fork collapse, a cause of DNA breaks and apoptosis.
  (3) While the fork regression/remodeling functions of SMARCAL1 have been investigated, its in vivo functions in replication stress and cancer are unclear.
  (4) Using a gamma radiation (IR)-induced replication stress T-cell lymphoma mouse model, we observed a significant inhibition of lymphomagenesis in mice lacking one or both alleles of Smarcal1.
  (5) Notably, a quarter of the Smarcal1-deficient mice did not develop tumors.
  (6) Moreover, hematopoietic stem/progenitor cells (HSPCs) and developing thymocytes in Smarcal1-deficient mice showed increased DNA damage and apoptosis during the proliferation burst following IR and an impaired ability to repopulate the thymus after IR.
  (7) Additionally, mice lacking Smarcal1 showed significant HSPC defects when challenged to respond to other replication stress stimuli.
  (8) Thus, our data reveal the critical function of the DNA replication stress response and, specifically, Smarcal1 in hematopoietic cell survival and tumor development.
  (9) Our results also provide important insight into the immunodeficiency observed in individuals with mutations in SMARCAL1 by suggesting that it is an HSPC defect.

[A5] Increased susceptibility to UV-induced skin carcinogenesis in polymerase eta-deficient mice.
  (0) Xeroderma pigmentosum variant (XPV) patients with mutations in the DNA polymerase eta (pol eta) gene are hypersensitive to sunlight and have greatly increased susceptibility to sunlight-induced skin cancer.
  (1) Consistent with the ability of Pol eta to efficiently bypass UV light-induced cyclobutane pyrimidine dimers, XPV cells lacking Pol eta have diminished capacity to replicate UV-damaged DNA and are sensitive to UV light-induced killing and mutagenesis.
  (2) To better understand these and other Pol eta functions, we generated Pol eta-deficient mice.
  (3) Mice homozygous for a null mutation in pol eta are viable, fertile, and do not show any obvious spontaneous defects during the first year of life.
  (4) However, fibroblasts derived from these mutant mice are sensitive to killing by exposure to UV light, and all Pol eta-deficient mice develop skin tumors after UV irradiation, in contrast to the wild-type littermate controls that did not develop such tumors.
  (5) These results and biochemical studies of translesion synthesis by mouse Pol eta indicate that Pol eta-dependent bypass of cyclobutane pyrimidine dimers suppresses UV light-induced skin cancer in mice.
  (6) Moreover, 37.5% of pol eta heterozygous mice also developed skin cancer during 5 months after a 5-month exposure to UV light, suggesting that humans who are heterozygous for mutations in pol eta may also have an increased risk of skin cancer.

## Claim 823

**Claim:** N348I mutations cause resistance to zidovudine (AZT).

**Obligations to label:**

- `823:o1` (core, subject): the subject is N348I mutations. Evidence must show: the study identifies N348I mutations. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `823:o2` (core, outcome): the outcome is resistance to zidovudine. Evidence must show: the study measures resistance to zidovudine. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `823:o3` (core, relation): mutations cause resistance to zidovudine. Evidence must show: resistance to zidovudine is caused by N348I mutations. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5

**Abstracts:**

[A1] N348I in the Connection Domain of HIV-1 Reverse Transcriptase Confers Zidovudine and Nevirapine Resistance
  (0) Background The catalytically active 66-kDa subunit of the human immunodeficiency virus type 1 (HIV-1) reverse transcriptase (RT) consists of DNA polymerase, connection, and ribonuclease H (RNase H) domains.
  (1) Almost all known RT inhibitor resistance mutations identified to date map to the polymerase domain of the enzyme.
  (2) However, the connection and RNase H domains are not routinely analysed in clinical samples and none of the genotyping assays available for patient management sequence the entire RT coding region.
  (3) The British Columbia Centre for Excellence in HIV/AIDS (the Centre) genotypes clinical isolates up to codon 400 in RT, and our retrospective statistical analyses of the Centre’s database have identified an N348I mutation in the RT connection domain in treatment-experienced individuals.
  (4) The objective of this multidisciplinary study was to establish the in vivo relevance of this mutation and its role in drug resistance.
  (5) Methods and Findings The prevalence of N348I in clinical isolates, the time taken for it to emerge under selective drug pressure, and its association with changes in viral load, specific drug treatment, and known drug resistance mutations was analysed from genotypes, viral loads, and treatment histories from the Centre’s database.
  (6) N348I increased in prevalence from below 1% in 368 treatmentnao ¨ve individuals to 12.1% in 1,009 treatment-experienced patients (p ¼ 7.7 3 10 � 12 ).
  (7) N348I appeared early in therapy and was highly associated with thymidine analogue mutations (TAMs) M41L and T215Y/F (p , 0.001), the lamivudine resistance mutations M184V/I (p , 0.001), and non-nucleoside RTI (NNRTI) resistance mutations K103N and Y181C/I (p , 0.001).
  (8) The association with TAMs and NNRTI resistance mutations was consistent with the selection of N348I in patients treated with regimens that included both zidovudine and nevirapine (odds ratio 2.62, 95% confidence interval 1.43–4.81).
  (9) The appearance of N348I was associated with a significant increase in viral load (p , 0.001), which was as large as the viral load increases observed for any of the TAMs.
  (10) However, this analysis did not account for the simultaneous selection of other RT or protease inhibitor resistance mutations on viral load.
  (11) To delineate the role of this mutation in RT inhibitor resistance, N348I was introduced into HIV-1 molecular clones containing different genetic backbones.
  (12) N348I decreased zidovudine susceptibility 2- to 4-fold in the context of wildtype HIV-1 or when combined with TAMs.
  (13) N348I also decreased susceptibility to nevirapine (7.4fold) and efavirenz (2.5-fold) and significantly potentiated resistance to these drugs when combined with K103N.
  (14) Biochemical analyses of recombinant RT containing N348I provide supporting evidence for the role of this mutation in zidovudine and NNRTI resistance and give some insight into the molecular mechanism of resistance.
  (15) Conclusions

[A2] N348I in HIV-1 reverse transcriptase can counteract the nevirapine-mediated bias toward RNase H cleavage during plus-strand initiation.
  (0) Drug resistance-associated mutations in HIV-1 reverse transcriptase (RT) can affect the balance between polymerase and ribonuclease H (RNase H) activities of the enzyme.
  (1) We have recently demonstrated that the N348I mutation in the connection domain causes selective dissociation from RNase H-competent complexes, whereas the functional integrity of the polymerase-competent complex remains largely unaffected.
  (2) N348I has been associated with resistance to the non-nucleoside RT inhibitor (NNRTI), nevirapine; however, a possible mechanism that links changes in RNase H activity to changes in NNRTI susceptibility remains to be established.
  (3) To address this problem, we consider recent findings suggesting that NNRTIs may affect the orientation of RT on its nucleic acid substrate and increase RNase H activity.
  (4) Here we demonstrate that RNase H-mediated primer removal is indeed more efficient in the presence of NNRTIs; however, the N348I mutant enzyme is able to counteract this effect.
  (5) Efavirenz, a tight binding inhibitor, restricts the influence of the mutation.
  (6) These findings provide strong evidence to suggest that N348I can thwart the inhibitory effects of nevirapine during initiation of (+)-strand DNA synthesis, which provides a novel mechanism for resistance.
  (7) The data are in agreement with clinical data, which demonstrate a stronger effect of N348I on susceptibility to nevirapine as compared with efavirenz.

[A3] Interferon alpha and zidovudine therapy in adult T-cell leukaemia lymphoma: response and outcome in 15 patients.
  (0) Adult T-cell leukaemia lymphoma (ATLL) is an aggressive disease caused by the human T-lymphotropic virus 1 (HTLV-I) with a short survival.
  (1) Responses to interferon alpha (IFN-alpha) and zidovudine (AZT) have been documented but not with long-term follow-up.
  (2) We treated 15 ATLL patients with IFN and AZT.
  (3) Eleven patients had acute ATLL, two had lymphoma and two smouldering ATLL, with progression.
  (4) The main features were: organomegaly (14), skin lesions (10), high white blood cell (WBC) count (11) and hypercalcaemia (9).
  (5) Eleven patients had previously received chemotherapy and one had received an autograft.
  (6) At the time of the study, seven patients had progressive disease and eight were in partial or complete clinical remission.
  (7) Responses (PR) lasting 2+ to 44+ months were seen in 67%; 26% did not respond (NR) and one patient was not evaluable.
  (8) Hypercalcaemia predicted a poor outcome but differences were not significant.
  (9) Eight of the 15 patients have died 3-41 months from diagnosis.
  (10) Median survival for the 15 patients was 18 months.
  (11) Survival of the NR ranged from 4 to 20 months; six PR patients are alive 8-82 months from diagnosis.
  (12) The differences in survival between NR (median: 6 months) and PR (55% of patients alive at 4 years) were statistically significant (P = 0.002).
  (13) In conclusion, IFN and AZT improves the outcome of ATLL patients and helps maintain responses.

[A4] A novel molecular mechanism of dual resistance to nucleoside and nonnucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors.
  (0) Recently, mutations in the connection subdomain (CN) and RNase H domain of HIV-1 reverse transcriptase (RT) were observed to exhibit dual resistance to nucleoside and nonnucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors (NRTIs and NNRTIs).
  (1) To elucidate the mechanism by which CN and RH mutations confer resistance to NNRTIs, we hypothesized that these mutations reduce RNase H cleavage and provide more time for the NNRTI to dissociate from the RT, resulting in the resumption of DNA synthesis and enhanced NNRTI resistance.
  (2) We observed that the effect of the reduction in RNase H cleavage on NNRTI resistance is dependent upon the affinity of each NNRTI to the RT and further influenced by the presence of NNRTI-binding pocket (BP) mutants.
  (3) D549N, Q475A, and Y501A mutants, which reduce RNase H cleavage, enhance resistance to nevirapine (NVP) and delavirdine (DLV), but not to efavirenz (EFV) and etravirine (ETR), consistent with their increase in affinity for RT.
  (4) Combining the D549N mutant with NNRTI BP mutants further increases NNRTI resistance from 3- to 30-fold, supporting the role of NNRTI-RT affinity in our NNRTI resistance model.
  (5) We also demonstrated that CNs from treatment-experienced patients, previously reported to enhance NRTI resistance, also reduce RNase H cleavage and enhance NNRTI resistance in the context of the patient RT pol domain or a wild-type pol domain.
  (6) Together, these results confirm key predictions of our NNRTI resistance model and provide support for a unifying mechanism by which CN and RH mutations can exhibit dual NRTI and NNRTI resistance.

[A5] Relapse specific mutations in NT5C2 in childhood acute lymphoblastic leukemia
  (0) Relapsed childhood acute lymphoblastic leukemia (ALL) carries a poor prognosis, despite intensive retreatment, owing to intrinsic drug resistance.
  (1) The biological pathways that mediate resistance are unknown.
  (2) Here, we report the transcriptome profiles of matched diagnosis and relapse bone marrow specimens from ten individuals with pediatric B-lymphoblastic leukemia using RNA sequencing.
  (3) Transcriptome sequencing identified 20 newly acquired, novel nonsynonymous mutations not present at initial diagnosis, with 2 individuals harboring relapse-specific mutations in the same gene, NT5C2, encoding a 5'-nucleotidase.
  (4) Full-exon sequencing of NT5C2 was completed in 61 further relapse specimens, identifying additional mutations in 5 cases.
  (5) Enzymatic analysis of mutant proteins showed that base substitutions conferred increased enzymatic activity and resistance to treatment with nucleoside analog therapies.
  (6) Clinically, all individuals who harbored NT5C2 mutations relapsed early, within 36 months of initial diagnosis (P = 0.03).
  (7) These results suggest that mutations in NT5C2 are associated with the outgrowth of drug-resistant clones in ALL.

## Claim 832

**Claim:** NFAT4 activation requires IP3R-mediated Ca2+ mobilization.

**Obligations to label:**

- `832:o1` (core, subject): NFAT4 is activated. Evidence must show: the study shows NFAT4 activation. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `832:o2` (core, relation): NFAT4 activation is mediated by IP3R. Evidence must show: IP3R is required for NFAT4 activation. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `832:o3` (core, outcome): Ca2+ mobilization occurs. Evidence must show: Ca2+ mobilization is observed. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5

**Abstracts:**

[A1] Control of NFAT Isoform Activation and NFAT-Dependent Gene Expression through Two Coincident and Spatially Segregated Intracellular Ca2+ Signals
  (0) Excitation-transcription coupling, linking stimulation at the cell surface to changes in nuclear gene expression, is conserved throughout eukaryotes.
  (1) How closely related coexpressed transcription factors are differentially activated remains unclear.
  (2) Here, we show that two Ca2+-dependent transcription factor isoforms, NFAT1 and NFAT4, require distinct sub-cellular InsP3 and Ca2+ signals for physiologically sustained activation.
  (3) NFAT1 is stimulated by sub-plasmalemmal Ca2+ microdomains, whereas NFAT4 additionally requires Ca2+ mobilization from the inner nuclear envelope by nuclear InsP3 receptors.
  (4) NFAT1 is rephosphorylated (deactivated) more slowly than NFAT4 in both cytoplasm and nucleus, enabling a more prolonged activation phase.
  (5) Oscillations in cytoplasmic Ca2+, long considered the physiological form of Ca2+ signaling, play no role in activating either NFAT protein.
  (6) Instead, effective sustained physiological activation of NFAT4 is tightly linked to oscillations in nuclear Ca2+.
  (7) Our results show how gene expression can be controlled by coincident yet geographically distinct Ca2+ signals, generated by a freely diffusible InsP3 message.

[A2] Frontrunners of T cell activation: Initial, localized Ca2+ signals mediated by NAADP and the type 1 ryanodine receptor
  (0) The activation of T cells is the fundamental on switch for the adaptive immune system.
  (1) Ca2+ signaling is essential for T cell activation and starts as initial, short-lived, localized Ca2+ signals.
  (2) The second messenger nicotinic acid adenine dinucleotide phosphate (NAADP) forms rapidly upon T cell activation and stimulates early Ca2+ signaling.
  (3) We developed a high-resolution imaging technique using multiple fluorescent Ca2+ indicator dyes to characterize these early signaling events and investigate the channels involved in NAADP-dependent Ca2+ signals.
  (4) In the first seconds of activation of either primary murine T cells or human Jurkat cells with beads coated with an antibody against CD3, we detected Ca2+ signals with diameters close to the limit of detection and that were close to the activation site at the plasma membrane.
  (5) In Jurkat cells in which the ryanodine receptor (RyR) was knocked down or in primary T cells from RyR1−/− mice, either these early Ca2+ signals were not detected or the number of signals was markedly reduced.
  (6) Local Ca2+ signals observed within 20 ms upon microinjection of Jurkat cells with NAADP were also sensitive to RyR knockdown.
  (7) In contrast, TRPM2 (transient receptor potential channel, subtype melastatin 2), a potential NAADP target channel, was not required for the formation of initial Ca2+ signals in primary T cells.
  (8) Thus, through our high-resolution imaging method, we characterized early Ca2+ release events in T cells and obtained evidence for the involvement of RyR and NAADP in such signals.

[A3] Local Ca2+ influx through Ca2+ release-activated Ca2+ (CRAC) channels stimulates production of an intracellular messenger and an intercellular pro-inflammatory signal.
  (0) Ca2+ entry through store-operated Ca2+ channels drives the production of the pro-inflammatory molecule leukotriene C4 (LTC4) from mast cells through a pathway involving Ca2+-dependent protein kinase C, mitogen-activated protein kinases ERK1/2, phospholipase A2, and 5-lipoxygenase.
  (1) Here we examine whether local Ca2+ influx through store-operated Ca2+ release-activated Ca2+ (CRAC) channels in the plasma membrane stimulates this signaling pathway.
  (2) Manipulating the amplitude and spatial extent of Ca2+ entry by altering chemical and electrical gradients for Ca2+ influx or changing the Ca2+ buffering of the cytoplasm all impacted on protein kinase C and ERK activation, generation of arachidonic acid and LTC4 secretion, with little change in the bulk cytoplasmic Ca2+ rise.
  (3) Similar bulk cytoplasmic Ca2+ concentrations were achieved when CRAC channels were activated in 0.25 mm external Ca2+ versus 2 mm Ca2+ and 100 nm La3+, an inhibitor of CRAC channels.
  (4) However, despite similar bulk cytoplasmic Ca2+, protein kinase C activation and LTC4 secretion were larger in 2 mm Ca2+ and La3+ than in 0.25 mm Ca2+, consistent with the central involvement of a subplasmalemmal Ca2+ rise.
  (5) The nonreceptor tyrosine kinase Syk coupled CRAC channel opening to protein kinase C and ERK activation.
  (6) Recombinant TRPC3 channels also activated protein kinase C, suggesting that subplasmalemmal Ca2+ rather than a microdomain exclusive to CRAC channels is the trigger.
  (7) Hence a subplasmalemmal Ca2+ increase in mast cells is highly versatile in that it triggers cytoplasmic responses through generation of intracellular messengers as well as long distance changes through increased secretion of paracrine signals.

[A4] Calicum microdomains form within neutrophils at the neutrophil–tumor cell synapse: role in antibody-dependent target cell apoptosis
  (0) Ca2+ messages are broadly important in cellular signal transduction.
  (1) In immune cells, Ca2+ signaling is an essential step in many forms of activation.
  (2) Neutrophil-mediated antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) is one form of leukocyte activation that plays an important role in tumor cell killing in vitro and in patient care.
  (3) Using fluorescence methodologies, we found that neutrophils exhibit Ca2+ signals during ADCC directed against breast fibrosarcoma cells.
  (4) Importantly, these signals were localized to Ca2+ microdomains at the neutrophil-to-tumor cell interface where they display dynamic features such as movement, fusion, and fission.
  (5) These signals were blocked by the intracellular Ca2+ buffer BAPTA.
  (6) At the neutrophil–tumor cell synapse, the neutrophil’s cytoplasm was enriched in STIM1, a crucial mediator of Ca2+ signaling, whereas the Ca2+-binding proteins calbindin and parvalbumin were not affected.
  (7) Our findings suggest that Ca2+ microdomains are due to an active signaling process.
  (8) As Ca2+ signals within neutrophils were necessary for specific tumor cell apoptosis, a central role of microdomains in leukocyte-mediated tumor cell destruction is indicated.

[A5] Inhibition of receptor-mediated calcium influx in T cells by unsaturated non-esterified fatty acids.
  (0) The effect of omega-3, omega-6 and omega-9 unsaturated fatty acids (UFAs) on receptor-mediated Ca2+ entry was investigated in a T-cell line (JURKAT) by using anti-CD3 antibodies (OKT3) to induce intracellular Ca2+ [( Ca2+]i) increase and Ca2+ influx.
  (1) All the UFAs, as well as Ni2+ ions and 12-O-tetradecanoylphorbol 13-acetate, decreased the OKT3-induced sustained [Ca2+]i increase to basal levels.
  (2) Although non-esterified fatty acids activate protein kinase C (PKC) [McPhail, Clayton & Snyderman (1984) Science 224, 622-624; Murakami, Chan & Routtenberg (1986) J. Biol.
  (3) Chem.
  (4) 261, 15424-15429], studies using H-7 and analysis of the PKC-dependent phosphorylation of 19 and 80 kDa marker substrates ruled out the involvement of PKC in UFA-induced inhibition of Ca2+ entry.
  (5) Flow-cytometry analysis showed that UFAs do not interfere with antibody-receptor binding.
  (6) BSA (0.2%, w/v) reversed the effect of UFAs after these fatty acids have decreased the OKT3-induced [Ca2+]i increase to basal levels.
  (7) The relevance of these findings and possible mechanisms for inhibition by UFAs of receptor-mediated Ca2+ influx were discussed.

## Claim 852

**Claim:** Non-invasive ventilation use should be decreased if there is inadequate response to conventional treatment.

**Obligations to label:**

- `852:o1` (core, subject): the intervention is non-invasive ventilation use. Evidence must show: the study measures or provides non-invasive ventilation use. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `852:o2` (core, outcome): the outcome is inadequate response to conventional treatment. Evidence must show: the study measures inadequate response to conventional treatment. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `852:o3` (core, relation): non-invasive ventilation use should be decreased if there is inadequate response to conventional treatment. Evidence must show: non-invasive ventilation use is decreased when there is inadequate response to conventional treatment. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5

**Abstracts:**

[A1] Non-invasive ventilation in chronic obstructive pulmonary disease patients: helmet versus facial mask
  (0) The helmet is a new interface with the potential of increasing the success rate of non-invasive ventilation by improving tolerance.
  (1) To perform a physiological comparison between the helmet and the conventional facial mask in delivering non-invasive ventilation in hypercapnic patients with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease.
  (2) Prospective, controlled, randomized study with cross-over design.
  (3) In 10 patients we evaluated gas exchange, inspiratory effort, patient–ventilator synchrony and patient tolerance after 30 min of non-invasive ventilation delivered either by helmet or facial mask; both trials were preceded by periods of spontaneous unassisted breathing.
  (4) Arterial blood gases, inspiratory effort, duration of diaphragm contraction and ventilator assistance, effort-to-support delays (at the beginning and at the end of inspiration), number of ineffective efforts, and patient comfort.
  (5) Non-invasive ventilation improved gas exchange (p< 0.05) and inspiratory effort (p< 0.01) with both interfaces.
  (6) The helmet, however, was less efficient than the mask in reducing inspiratory effort (p< 0.05) and worsened the patient–ventilator synchrony, as indicated by the longer delays to trigger on (p< 0.05) and cycle off (p< 0.05) the mechanical assistance and by the number of ineffective efforts (p< 0.005).
  (7) Patient comfort was no different with the two interfaces.
  (8) Helmet and facial mask were equally tolerated and both were effective in ameliorating gas exchange and decreasing inspiratory effort.
  (9) The helmet, however, was less efficient in decreasing inspiratory effort and worsened the patient–ventilator interaction.

[A2] Cost effectiveness of ward based non-invasive ventilation for acute exacerbations of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease: economic analysis of randomised controlled trial.
  (0) OBJECTIVE To evaluate the cost effectiveness of standard treatment with and without the addition of ward based non-invasive ventilation in patients admitted to hospital with an acute exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease.
  (1) DESIGN Incremental cost effectiveness analysis of a randomised controlled trial.
  (2) SETTING Medical wards in 14 hospitals in the United Kingdom.
  (3) PARTICIPANTS The trial comprised 236 patients admitted to hospital with an acute exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease and mild to moderate acidosis (pH 7.25-7.35) secondary to respiratory failure.
  (4) The economic analysis compared the costs of treatment that these patients received after randomisation.
  (5) MAIN OUTCOME MEASURE Incremental cost per in-hospital death.
  (6) RESULTS 24/118 died in the group receiving standard treatment and 12/118 in the group receiving non-invasive ventilation (P=0.05).
  (7) Allocation to the group receiving non-invasive ventilation was associated with a reduction in costs of 49362 pounds sterling (78741 dollars; 73109 euros), mainly through reduced use of intensive care units.
  (8) The incremental cost effectiveness ratio was -645 pounds sterling per death avoided (95% confidence interval -2310 pounds sterling to 386 pounds sterling), indicating a dominant (more effective and less costly) strategy.
  (9) Modelling of these data indicates that a typical UK hospital providing a non-invasive ventilation service will avoid six deaths and three to nine admissions to intensive care units per year, with an associated cost reduction of 12000-53000 pounds sterling per year.
  (10) CONCLUSIONS Non-invasive ventilation is a highly cost effective treatment that both reduced total costs and improved mortality in hospital.

[A3] A comparison of continuous and bi-level positive airway pressure non-invasive ventilation in patients with acute cardiogenic pulmonary oedema: a meta-analysis
  (0) IntroductionWe conducted the present study to investigate the potential beneficial and adverse effects of continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) compared with bi-level positive airway pressure (BiPAP) noninvasive ventilation in patients with cardiogenic pulmonary oedema.
  (1) MethodWe included randomized controlled studies comparing CPAP and BiPAP treatment in patients with cardiogenic pulmonary oedema from the Cochrane Controlled Trials Register (2005 issue 3), and EMBASE and MEDLINE databases (1966 to 1 December 2005), without language restriction.
  (2) Two reviewers reviewed the quality of the studies and independently performed data extraction.
  (3) ResultsSeven randomized controlled studies, including a total of 290 patients with cardiogenic pulmonary oedema, were considered.
  (4) The hospital mortality (relative risk [RR] 0.76, 95% confidence interval [CI] 0.32–1.78; P = 0.52; I2 = 0%) and risk for requiring invasive ventilation (RR 0.80, 95% CI 0.33–1.94; P = 0.62; I2 = 0%) were not significantly different between patients treated with CPAP and those treated with BiPAP.
  (5) Stratifying studies that used either fixed or titrated pressure during BiPAP treatment and studies involving patients with or without hypercapnia did not change the results.
  (6) The duration of noninvasive ventilation required until the pulmonary oedema resolved (weighted mean difference [WMD] in hours = 3.65, 95% CI -12.12 to +19.43; P = 0.65, I2 = 0%) and length of hospital stay (WMD in days = -0.04, 95% CI -2.57 to +2.48; P = 0.97, I2 = 0%) were also not significantly different between the two groups.
  (7) Based on the limited data available, there was an insignificant trend toward an increase in new onset acute myocardial infarction in patients treated with BiPAP (RR 2.10, 95% CI 0.91–4.84; P = 0.08; I2 = 25.3%).ConclusionBiPAP does not offer any significant clinical benefits over CPAP in patients with acute cardiogenic pulmonary oedema.
  (8) Until a large randomized controlled trial shows significant clinical benefit and cost-effectiveness of BiPAP versus CPAP in patients with acute cardiogenic pulmonary oedema, the choice of modality will depend mainly on the equipment available.

[A4] Noninvasive ventilation for treatment of acute respiratory failure in patients undergoing solid organ transplantation: a randomized trial.
  (0) CONTEXT Noninvasive ventilation (NIV) has been associated with lower rates of endotracheal intubation in populations of patients with acute respiratory failure.
  (1) OBJECTIVE To compare NIV with standard treatment using supplemental oxygen administration to avoid endotracheal intubation in recipients of solid organ transplantation with acute hypoxemic respiratory failure.
  (2) DESIGN AND SETTING Prospective randomized study conducted at a 14-bed, general intensive care unit of a university hospital.
  (3) PATIENTS Of 238 patients who underwent solid organ transplantation from December 1995 to October 1997, 51 were treated for acute respiratory failure.
  (4) Of these, 40 were eligible and 20 were randomized to each group.
  (5) INTERVENTION Noninvasive ventilation vs standard treatment with supplemental oxygen administration.
  (6) MAIN OUTCOME MEASURES The need for endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation at any time during the study, complications not present on admission, duration of ventilatory assistance, length of hospital stay, and intensive care unit mortality.
  (7) RESULTS The 2 groups were similar at study entry.
  (8) Within the first hour of treatment, 14 patients (70%) in the NIV group, and 5 patients (25%) in the standard treatment group improved their ratio of the PaO2 to the fraction of inspired oxygen (FIO2).
  (9) Over time, a sustained improvement in PaO2 to FIO2 was noted in 12 patients (60%) in the NIV group, and in 5 patients (25%) randomized to standard treatment (P = .03).
  (10) The use of NIV was associated with a significant reduction in the rate of endotracheal intubation (20% vs 70%; P = .002), rate of fatal complications (20% vs 50%; P = .05), length of stay in the intensive care unit by survivors (mean [SD] days, 5.5 [3] vs 9 [4]; P = .03), and intensive care unit mortality (20% vs 50%; P = .05).
  (11) Hospital mortality did not differ.
  (12) CONCLUSIONS These results indicate that transplantation programs should consider NIV in the treatment of selected recipients of transplantation with acute respiratory failure.

[A5] Ethical dilemmas in a randomized trial of asthma treatment: can Bayesian statistical analysis explain the results?
  (0) OBJECTIVES The original objective was to determine whether the use of bilevel positive airway pressure (BiPAP) ventilation would reduce the need for endotracheal intubation, the length of hospital stay, and hospital charges in patients with status asthmaticus.
  (1) The development of physician treatment bias made patient enrollment difficult.
  (2) The article subsequently describes the use of Bayesian statistics to explain study results when this bias occurs.
  (3) METHODS This study was a prospective, randomized controlled clinical trial conducted over a 34.5-month period at an urban university hospital with an emergency department census of 94,000 annual visits.
  (4) Patients remaining in status asthmaticus after initial standard treatment with inhaled beta-agonists and steroids were randomized to receive BiPAP ventilation plus standard treatment versus standard treatment alone (non-BiPAP), with intubation for either group as needed.
  (5) Patients with concurrent cardiac or other pulmonary diseases were excluded.
  (6) The primary outcome measures were endotracheal intubation rate and length of hospital stay.
  (7) Secondary outcome measures included vital signs (respiratory rate, pulse rate, blood pressure), changes in expiratory peak flow, changes in pulse oximetry values, and hospital charges.
  (8) Data were analyzed using Fisher's exact test, Mann-Whitney tests, and Bayesian statistics.
  (9) For patients enrolled in the study more than once, data analysis was performed on the first enrollment only.
  (10) RESULTS Nineteen patients were enrolled in the BiPAP group and 16 patients in the non-BiPAP group.
  (11) Patients were frequently enrolled more than once and the data from the subsequent enrollments were excluded from the analysis.
  (12) A marked decrease in enrollment, due to physician treatment bias, led to a premature termination of the study.
  (13) Demographics showed that the groups were similar in age, sex, initial peak flow rate, and arterial blood gas measurements.
  (14) There was a 7.3% increase (95% CI = -22 to +45) in the intubation rate in the non-BiPAP group (n = 2) compared with that for the BiPAP group (n = 1).
  (15) No significant difference was seen in length of hospital stay or hospital charges, although there was a favorable trend toward the BiPAP group.
  (16) Complications encountered in the BiPAP group included one patient with discomfort associated with the nasal BiPAP mask.
  (17) Bayesian analysis demonstrated that in order for the collected data to be convincing at the 95% confidence level, the prior conviction among treating physicians that BiPAP was a successful treatment modality would have had to be 98.9%.
  (18) CONCLUSIONS In this study, BiPAP appeared to have no deleterious effects in patients with status asthmaticus, with a trend toward decreased endotracheal intubation rate, decreased length of hospital stay, and decreased hospital charges.
  (19) Although further study with more patients is needed to determine the clinical and statistical significance of this intervention, ethical concerns regarding withholding BiPAP treatment from the patients in the control group forced a premature termination of the study in the authors' institution.

## Claim 870

**Claim:** Obesity decreases life quality.

**Obligations to label:**

- `870:o1` (core, subject): the subject is obesity. Evidence must show: the study measures or defines obesity. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `870:o2` (core, outcome): the outcome is life quality. Evidence must show: the study measures life quality. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5
- `870:o3` (core, relation): obesity decreases life quality. Evidence must show: life quality is lower in individuals with obesity. Audited on: A1 A2 A3 A4 A5

**Abstracts:**

[A1] Psychological Outcome 4 Years after Restrictive Bariatric Surgery
  (0) Extreme obesity is associated with severe psychiatric and somatic comorbidity and impairment of psychosocial functioning.
  (1) Bariatric surgery is the most effective treatment not only with regard to weight loss but also with obesity-associated illnesses.
  (2) Health-related psychological and psychosocial variables have been increasingly considered as important outcome variables of bariatric surgery.
  (3) However, the long-term impact of bariatric surgery on psychological and psychosocial functioning is largely unclear.
  (4) The aim of this study was to evaluate the relationship between the course of weight and psychological variables including depression, anxiety, health-related quality of life (HRQOL), and self-esteem up to 4 years after obesity surgery.
  (5) By standardized questionnaires prior to (T1) and 1 year (T2), 2 years (T3), and 4 years (T4) after surgery, 148 patients (47 males (31.8 %), 101 females (68.2 %), mean age 38.8 ± 10.2 years) were assessed.
  (6) On average, participants lost 24.6 % of their initial weight 1 year after surgery, 25.1 % after 2 years, and 22.3 % after 4 years.
  (7) Statistical analysis revealed significant improvements in depressive symptoms, physical dimension of quality of life, and self-esteem with peak improvements 1 year after surgery.
  (8) These improvements were largely maintained.
  (9) Significant correlations between weight loss and improvements in depression, physical aspects of HRQOL (T2, T3, and T4), and self-esteem (T3) were observed.
  (10) Corresponding to the considerable weight loss after bariatric surgery, important aspects of mental health improved significantly during the 4-year follow-up period.
  (11) However, parallel to weight regain, psychological improvements showed a slow but not significant decline over time.

[A2] Functional outcomes of multi-condition collaborative care and successful ageing: results of randomised trial
